The '''2022 United States Senate elections''' were held on November 8, 2022, concurrently with other midterm elections at the federal, state, and local levels. Regularly scheduled elections were held for 34 of the 100 seats in the [[United States Senate|U.S. Senate]], the winners of which would serve six-year terms beginning with the [[118th United States Congress]]. Two special elections were held to complete unexpired terms. While pundits considered the [[Republican Party (United States)|Republican Party]] a slight favorite to gain control of the Senate, the Democrats outperformed expectations and expanded the majority they had held since 2021,<ref name="FiveThirtyEight 2022">{{cite web |date=2022-11-08 |title=2022 Election: Live Analysis and Results |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/live-blog/2022-midterm-election/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221108150157/https://fivethirtyeight.com/live-blog/2022-midterm-election/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=November 8, 2022 |access-date=2022-11-09 |website=FiveThirtyEight}}</ref><ref name="Hulse 2022">{{cite news |last=Hulse |first=Carl |date=2022-12-07 |title=Democrats Didn't Just Win Georgia. They Secured a Firmer Grip on the Senate |newspaper=The New York Times |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2022/12/06/us/democrats-georgia-senate-majority.html |access-date=2022-12-07 |issn=0362-4331}}</ref> gaining a seat for a functioning 51–49 majority.

Senators are divided into three classes whose terms are staggered so that a different class is elected every other year. All 34 Class 3 Senate seats, last elected in 2016, were up for election in 2022. Before the elections, Class 3 consisted of 14 Democrats and 20 Republicans. Special elections were concurrently held in California, to fill Vice President [[Kamala Harris]]'s unexpired Senate term ending in 2022,<ref>{{cite news |last=Wilson |first=Reid |date=September 28, 2021 |title=California rule change means Padilla faces extra election |work=The Hill|url=https://thehill.com/homenews/campaign/574242-california-rule-change-means-padilla-faces-extra-election |url-status=live |access-date=September 28, 2021 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210928225230/https://thehill.com/homenews/campaign/574242-california-rule-change-means-padilla-faces-extra-election |archive-date=September 28, 2021}}</ref> and in Oklahoma, to fill the remaining four years of [[Jim Inhofe]]'s unexpired term.<ref>{{cite web |last1=Beavers |first1=Olivia |last2=Everett |first2=Burgess |date=February 24, 2022 |title=Inhofe to retire from Senate, teeing up special election in Oklahoma |url=https://www.politico.com/news/2022/02/24/inhofe-to-retire-from-senate-teeing-up-special-election-in-oklahoma-00011534 |access-date=November 9, 2022 |website=Politico}}</ref> Five Republican senators and one Democratic senator retired instead of seeking re-election; 15 Republicans and 13 Democrats ran for re-election. Before the elections, Democrats had held a majority in the Senate since January 20, 2021. There were 48 Democratic and two independent senators who caucused with them; Harris's tie-breaking vote as vice president gave Democrats control of the chamber.<ref name="FiveThirtyEight 2022"/>

While Republicans appeared slightly favored in several competitive races, a [[red wave election]] did not materialize.<ref name="Hounshell 2022">{{cite news |last=Hounshell |first=Blake |date=2022-11-09 |title=Five Takeaways From a Red Wave That Didn't Reach the Shore |newspaper=The New York Times |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2022/11/09/us/politics/midterm-elections-takeaways.html |access-date=2022-11-09 |issn=0362-4331}}</ref><ref name="Knowles & Scherer 2022">{{cite news |last1=Knowles |first1=Hannah |last2=Scherer |first2=Michael |date=November 9, 2022 |title=Democrats show strength, leaving fight for control of Congress unresolved |newspaper=The Washington Post |url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/elections/2022/11/08/house-senate-race/ |access-date=November 9, 2022}}</ref><ref name="McGraw 2022">{{cite web |last=McGraw |first=Meridith |date=November 9, 2022 |title=Trump's biggest midterm bets don't pay out |url=https://www.politico.com/news/2022/11/09/trump-endorsed-candidates-2022-election-results |access-date=November 9, 2022 |website=Politico}}</ref> Democrats gained a seat, in Pennsylvania where Democrat [[John Fetterman]] won the election to succeed retiring Republican Pat Toomey.<ref>{{cite web |date=2022-11-08 |title=Democrat John Fetterman wins US Senate race in Pennsylvania |url=https://apnews.com/article/pennsylvania-senate-race-2022-midterm-elections-93709b5b7ab4cef658f45751cd76c090 |access-date=2022-11-10 |website=AP News |publisher=Associated Press}}</ref> All incumbents won re-election, and all other open seats besides Pennsylvania were held by the same party as the retiring senator. For the first time since the ratification of the [[Seventeenth Amendment to the United States Constitution|17th Amendment]], no incumbent lost a U.S. Senate primary or general election.<ref name=":0">{{cite web |last=Girous|first=Greg|date=2022-12-07|title=Warnock Win Seals Perfect 2022 for Senators Seeking Re-election |url=https://news.bloomberglaw.com/us-law-week/warnock-win-seals-perfect-2022-for-senators-seeking-re-election |access-date=2022-12-12 |website=Bloomberg Law}}</ref>{{Efn |name="1914election"|In the 1914 U.S. Senate elections, no incumbent senator lost a general election, but two senators, one each from Kansas and South Dakota, lost renomination in their primary elections.}}

The better-than-expected performance of Democrats has been attributed to several factors,<ref name="Koerth 2022">{{cite web |last=Koerth |first=Maggie |date=2022-11-18 |title=So You Think You Can Explain The Election |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/election-explanations-are-hard/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221118152513/https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/election-explanations-are-hard/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=November 18, 2022 |access-date=2022-11-20 |website=FiveThirtyEight}}</ref> including the issue of abortion after ''[[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization]]'',<ref name="Radcliffe 2022">{{cite web |last=Radcliffe |first=Mary |date=2022-11-17 |title=Abortion Was Always Going To Impact The Midterms |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/abortion-was-always-going-to-impact-the-midterms/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221117110110/https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/abortion-was-always-going-to-impact-the-midterms/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=November 17, 2022 |access-date=2022-11-20 |website=FiveThirtyEight}}</ref> the role of [[Donald Trump]], and alleged extremism or [[Election denial movement in the United States|election denialism]] among Republicans.<ref name="Silver 2022">{{cite web |last=Silver |first=Nate |date=2022-11-09 |title=Candidate Quality Mattered |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/candidate-quality-mattered/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221109221033/https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/candidate-quality-mattered/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=November 9, 2022 |access-date=2022-11-10 |website=FiveThirtyEight}}</ref><ref name="Levine & Pilkington 2022">{{cite news |last1=Levine|first1=Sam|last2=Pilkington|first2=Ed|date=2022-11-09 |title=US midterm voters reject election deniers who support Trump's false claim |url=https://www.theguardian.com/us-news/2022/nov/09/republican-election-deniers-trump-mastriano-karamo-finchem-marchant-midterms |access-date=2022-12-12 |work=The Guardian}}</ref><ref name="Wolf 2022">{{cite news |last=Wolf |first=Zachary B. |date=2022-11-14 |title=These Republicans are admitting the party has an extremism problem |publisher=CNN |url=https://edition.cnn.com/2022/11/14/politics/republican-party-extremism-problem-what-matters/index.html |access-date=2022-11-30}}</ref> The 2022 election cycle was the first time in U.S. history in which multiple Senate races in the same year were contested between two Black nominees (Georgia and South Carolina).<ref>{{cite web|last1=Amy|first1=Jeff|last2=Thanawala|first2=Sudhin|title=In Georgia, 2 Black candidates to compete for Senate seat|website=The Associated Press|date=2022-05-28|url=https://apnews.com/article/2022-midterm-elections-raphael-warnock-herschel-walker-5e8beec03c05d64240343073255725d2|access-date=2022-12-22}}</ref>{{efn|Three previous elections have taken place in which both major-party nominees were Black: the [[2004 U.S. Senate election in Illinois]], the [[2014 U.S. Senate special election in South Carolina]], and the [[2016 U.S. Senate election in South Carolina]].}}

== Partisan composition ==
All 34 [[Class 3 senators]] were up for election in 2022; before [[Election Day (United States)|Election Day]], Class 3 consisted of 14 Democrats and 20 Republicans, including a seat in California held by an interim appointee up for a [[List of special elections to the United States Senate|special election]]. Additionally, a special election was held for a Class 2 seat in Oklahoma. Of the senators not up for election, 34 were Democrats, 29 were Republicans, and two were independent members who caucused with the [[Senate Democrats]].<ref name="FiveThirtyEight 2022"/>

In recent cycles, partisanship in Senate elections has much more closely matched partisanship in presidential elections, and the number of senators representing states won recently by presidential candidates of the opposite party has dwindled. In 2018, Democrats were defending 10 seats in states that [[Donald Trump]] won in the [[2016 U.S. presidential election]],{{efn|Democratic incumbents were reelected in Michigan, Montana, Ohio, Pennsylvania, West Virginia, and Wisconsin, while Republicans flipped Florida, Indiana, Missouri, and North Dakota.}} while Republicans held only one seat in a state that [[Hillary Clinton]] won in 2016 (Nevada, which Democrats flipped). In contrast, Democrats in this cycle held no seats in states that Trump won in the [[2020 U.S. presidential election]], while Republicans were defending only two seats in states [[Joe Biden]] won in 2020 (Pennsylvania, which Democrats flipped, and Wisconsin, which Republicans narrowly held).<ref name="FiveThirtyEight 2022"/>

Democrats had held a majority in the Senate since January 20, 2021, following the party's twin victories in the run-offs for Georgia's [[2020–21 United States Senate election in Georgia|regular]] and [[2020–21 United States Senate special election in Georgia|special]] 2020–2021 Senate elections, and the inauguration of Harris as vice president. While many pundits believed Republicans had a strong chance to flip control of the chamber, a [[red wave election]] did not materialize.<ref name="Hounshell 2022"/><ref name="Knowles & Scherer 2022"/><ref name="McGraw 2022"/> Instead, Democrats performed better than expected in many states, including Pennsylvania, New Hampshire, and Ohio.<ref name="FiveThirtyEight 2022"/><ref name="Yglesias 2022">{{cite news |last=Yglesias |first=Matthew |date=2022-11-09 |title=Democrats are doing far better than expected. How come? |work=The Guardian |url=https://www.theguardian.com/commentisfree/2022/nov/09/democrats-did-far-better-than-expected-how-come |access-date=2022-11-09}}</ref><ref name="Ax & Reid 2022">{{cite news |last1=Ax |first1=Joseph|last2=Reid|first2=Tim |date=2022-11-10 |title=U.S. Senate up for grabs as Republicans move toward House majority |work=Reuters |url=https://www.reuters.com/world/us/republicans-close-us-house-majority-senate-still-up-grabs-2022-11-10/ |access-date=2022-11-10}}</ref> In Colorado, where some Republican strategists hoped for a competitive race, [[Michael Bennet]] won re-election handily, and in New Hampshire, another hopeful Republican target, [[Maggie Hassan]] ran ahead of Biden's [[2020 United States presidential election in New Hampshire|2020 margin in the state]].<ref name="Yglesias 2022"/><ref name="Ax & Reid 2022"/> Democrats also beat expectations in [[Rust Belt]] states; although [[Tim Ryan (Ohio politician)|Tim Ryan]] lost in Ohio, his performance in the race had a [[coattail effect]] that boosted Democrats in competitive House districts in the state,<ref name="Skelley 2022">{{cite web |last=Skelley |first=Geoffrey |date=2022-11-13 |title=A Blue Nevada Means Democrats Will Keep Control Of The Senate |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/a-blue-nevada-means-democrats-will-keep-control-of-the-senate/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221113030628/https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/a-blue-nevada-means-democrats-will-keep-control-of-the-senate/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=November 13, 2022 |access-date=2022-11-20 |website=FiveThirtyEight}}</ref> and in Pennsylvania, where [[John Fetterman]] defeated Trump-endorsee [[Mehmet Oz]], vulnerable House Democrats also benefitted from strong Democratic performance at the top of the ticket.<ref>{{cite web |last=Wren |first=Adam |date=2022-11-09 |title=Democrats fortify their blue wall — and Electoral College math — for 2024 |url=https://www.politico.com/news/2022/11/09/democrats-blue-wall-electoral-college-2024-00066127 |access-date=2022-12-01 |website=Politico}}</ref> Fetterman improved upon Biden's [[2020 United States presidential election in Pennsylvania|2020 results]] from white voters without a college degree.<ref>{{cite web |last=Skelley |first=Geoffrey |date=2022-12-19 |title=The Voters Who Helped Democrats Keep the Senate |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/biden-coalition-2022-senate-democrats/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221219110653/https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/biden-coalition-2022-senate-democrats/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=December 19, 2022 |access-date=2022-12-20 |website=FiveThirtyEight}}</ref> In Georgia's first round, [[Raphael Warnock]] improved upon his margin from 2020–2021 and finished first,<ref name="FiveThirtyEight 2022"/> before winning by 3 percentage points in the December runoff.<ref>{{cite web |date=2022-12-06 |title=Georgia Senate Runoff: How Raphael Warnock Secured Democrats A 51st Senate Seat |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/live-blog/georgia-senate-warnock-walker/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221207001900/https://fivethirtyeight.com/live-blog/georgia-senate-warnock-walker/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=December 7, 2022 |access-date=2022-12-09 |website=FiveThirtyEight}}</ref>

Democrats' strong performance has been attributed to, among other factors,<ref name="Koerth 2022"/> backlash to abortion-rights restrictions following the [[U.S. Supreme Court]]'s June 2022 decision in ''[[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization]]'' that overturned ''[[Roe v. Wade]]'',<ref name="Radcliffe 2022"/> negative reaction to Republican extremism and election denialism,<ref name="Levine & Pilkington 2022"/><ref name="Wolf 2022"/> better candidate quality among Democrats than Republicans,<ref name="Silver 2022"/> and youth turnout and [[vote splitting]] in key races.<ref>{{cite news |last=Salam |first=Erum |date=2022-11-11 |title=Analysis: Young voters hailed as key to Democratic successes in midterms |url=https://www.theguardian.com/us-news/2022/nov/11/young-voters-us-midterms-democratic-youth |work=The Guardian |access-date=2022-11-13}}</ref><ref>{{cite web |date=2022-11-11 |title=Analysis Reveals How Abortion Boosted Democratic Candidates in Tuesday's Midterm Election |url=https://www.kff.org/other/press-release/analysis-reveals-how-abortion-boosted-democratic-candidates-in-tuesdays-midterm-election/ |access-date=2022-12-03 |website=KFF}}</ref> Some Republicans blamed Trump for the party's underwhelming showing, citing the underperformance of candidates he endorsed such as [[Herschel Walker]] in Georgia and Oz in Pennsylvania.<ref>{{cite news |last=Ax |first=Joseph |date=2022-11-10 |title=U.S. Senate up for grabs as Republicans move toward House majority |work=Reuters |url=https://www.reuters.com/world/us/republicans-close-us-house-majority-senate-still-up-grabs-2022-11-10/ |access-date=2022-11-10}}</ref><ref>{{cite web |last=Skelley |first=Geoffrey |date=2022-11-28 |title=Few Midterm Voters Backed Different Parties For Senate And Governor |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/few-midterm-voters-backed-different-parties-for-senate-and-governor/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221128191813/https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/few-midterm-voters-backed-different-parties-for-senate-and-governor/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=November 28, 2022 |access-date=2022-12-01 |website=FiveThirtyEight}}</ref><ref>{{cite web |last=Rogers |first=Kaleigh |date=2022-11-29 |title=When Democracy Was On The Ballot In 2022, Voters Usually Chose It |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/rogers-democracy-on-ballot-1129/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221129125453/https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/rogers-democracy-on-ballot-1129/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=November 29, 2022 |access-date=2022-12-01 |website=FiveThirtyEight}}</ref> Democrats won full terms in the Class 3 Senate seats in Arizona and Pennsylvania for the first time since the [[1962 United States Senate elections|1962 elections]].

The 2022 election cycle was the first time since the [[2006 United States Senate elections|2006 Senate elections]] that Democrats made net gains in a midterm year. Additionally, the 2022 cycle tied with the [[1990 United States Senate elections|1990 elections]] for the lowest number of party flips, at only 1 seat each. This was only the third election in U.S. history (after 1914 and [[1934 United States Senate elections|1934]]) where the opposition party failed to flip any Senate seats. It is the most recent election cycle in which the president's party gained Senate seats and simultaneously lost House seats in a midterm, which also occurred in 1914, 1962, 1970, and 2018; it was the first midterm in which Democrats did so since 1962.<ref name="Thompson 2022">{{cite magazine |last=Thompson |first=Derek |date=2022-11-09 |title=Democrats Might Have Pulled Off the Biggest Midterm Shock in Decades |magazine=The Atlantic |url=https://www.theatlantic.com/newsletters/archive/2022/11/democrats-biggest-midterm-shock-polls/672055/ |access-date=2022-12-07}}</ref> It was a historically good cycle for incumbents;<ref>{{cite news |last=Ulloa |first=Jazmine |date=2022-11-11|title=Despite Discontent, Midterm Voters Did Not Kick Out Incumbents |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2022/11/11/us/politics/midterm-incumbent-results.html |newspaper=The New York Times |access-date=2022-11-11|issn=0362-4331}}</ref> it was the first time ever since the ratification of the [[Seventeenth Amendment to the United States Constitution|17th Amendment]], which mandated the popular election of U.S. senators, in which no incumbents were defeated for either a primary or general election.<ref name=":0" />{{Efn |name="1914election"|In the 1914 U.S. Senate elections, no incumbent senator lost a general election, but two senators, one each from Kansas and South Dakota, lost renomination in their primary elections.}} [[Maggie Hassan]] (New Hampshire), [[Ron Johnson]] (Wisconsin), [[Mark Kelly]] (Arizona), [[Catherine Cortez Masto|Catherine Cortez-Masto]] (Nevada), [[Lisa Murkowski]] (Alaska), and Warnock (Georgia) faced competitive races but were all re-elected.<ref name="FiveThirtyEight 2022"/><ref name="Hulse 2022"/>

== Change in composition ==
Each block represents one of the one hundred seats in the U.S. Senate. "D{{Sub|#}}" is a Democratic senator, "I{{Sub|#}}" is an independent senator, and "R{{Sub|#}}" is a Republican senator. They are arranged so the parties are separated and a majority is clear by crossing the middle.

=== Before the elections ===
Each block indicates an incumbent senator's actions going into the election.

=== Beginning of the first session ===

== Final pre-election predictions ==
Several sites and individuals publish predictions of competitive seats. These predictions look at factors such as the strength of the [[incumbent]] (if the incumbent is running for re-election) and the other candidates and the state's partisan lean (reflected in part by the state's [[Cook Partisan Voting Index]] rating). The predictions assign ratings to each seat, indicating the predicted advantage that a party had in winning that seat. Most election predictors use:
* "<u>tossup</u>" / "<u>battleground</u>": no advantage 
* "<u>tilt</u>" (used by some predictors): minimal, smallest advantage
* "<u>lean</u>": slight advantage
* "<u>likely</u>": significant, but surmountable, advantage
* "<u>safe</u>" or "<u>solid</u>": near-certain chance of victory<!-- Colors and sorting:

In many cases, the incumbent has yet to say their intention of running for another term. If an incumbent intends to retire then place "<br />{{Small|(Retiring)}}" after the incumbent's name.

See [[Template:USRaceRating]] for how to apply rating.

-->

===Post-election changes===
One Democrat died during the 118th Congress and was replaced by a Democratic appointee. One Republican resigned shortly after the start of the 118th Congress and was replaced by a Republican appointee. One Democrat resigned during the 118th Congress and was replaced by a Democratic appointee. In Nebraska, a [[2024 United States Senate special election in Nebraska|2024 special election]] was held prior to the [[2026 United States Senate elections|2026 Senate elections]] for the remainder of the Class 2 term, where Republican appointee Pete Ricketts won the special election to finish the term. In [[2024 United States Senate elections in California|California]] and [[2024 United States Senate election in New Jersey|New Jersey]], regular scheduled elections were held in the [[2024 United States Senate elections|2024 Senate elections]] for the Class 1 terms, where Democrat Adam Schiff won the California election to succeed Democratic appointee Laphonza Butler, who did not seek election; and in New Jersey, where Andy Kim won to succeed Democratic appointee George Helmy, who did not seek election.

==Race summary==
=== Special elections during the preceding Congress ===
In each special election, the winner's term can begin immediately after their election is certified by their state's government. In cases where a resignation has been previously announced, the new senator's term can begin once the previous senator's resignation is submitted officially.

Elections are sorted by date, then state.

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Alabama}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Alabama|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Alabama}}
Six-term Republican [[Richard Shelby]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Alabama|re-elected in 2016]] with 64% of the vote. On February 8, 2021, Shelby announced that he would not seek re-election to a seventh term.<ref>{{cite news |last1=Hulse |first1=Carl |title=Shelby, Veteran Senator from Alabama, Won't Seek Seventh Term |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2021/02/08/us/senator-richard-shelby-retire.html |access-date=February 8, 2021 |work=[[The New York Times]] |date=February 8, 2021}}</ref> [[Katie Britt]], Shelby's former chief of staff,<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.alreporter.com/2021/06/08/katie-britt-announces-u-s-senate-candidacy/|title = Katie Britt announces U.S. Senate candidacy|date = June 8, 2021|author=Walker, Charlie|work=Alabama Political Reporter}}</ref> and [[Mo Brooks]], a six-term [[U.S. representative]], finished ahead of businesswoman Karla DuPriest, former Army pilot and author [[Michael Durant]], and author Jake Schafer in the first round of the Republican primary election, with Britt going on to defeat Brooks in a runoff.<ref>{{cite news |title=Qualifying for 2022 election ends Friday, Jan. 28 |url=https://www.andalusiastarnews.com/2022/01/25/qualifying-for-2022-election-ends-friday-jan-28/ |access-date=January 27, 2022 |publisher=The Andalusia Star News|author=Blankenship, Robert|date=January 25, 2022}}</ref> [[Perennial candidate]] [[Will Boyd]]<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.alreporter.com/2022/01/31/will-boyd-announces-u-s-senate-run/|title=Will Boyd announces U.S. Senate run|last=Burkhalter|first=Eddie|work=Alabama Political Reporter|date=January 31, 2022|access-date=February 2, 2022}}</ref> defeated former [[Brighton, Alabama|Brighton]] mayor Brandaun Dean<ref>{{cite web|url=https://1819news.com/news/item/120c52d4-a327-4608-b74e-31682af7ad0d/democratic-senate-candidate-says-that-he-will-be-the-%E2%80%9Cmost-anti-poverty-and-pro-b|title=Democratic Senate candidate says that he will be the "most anti-poverty and pro Black candidate"|last=Moseley|first=Brandon|work=1819 News|date=January 31, 2022|access-date=February 2, 2022}}</ref> and Lanny Jackson<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.al.com/news/2022/01/alabama-republican-candidates-far-outnumber-democrats-as-qualifying-for-may-24-primary-wraps-up.html|title=Alabama Republican candidates far outnumber Democrats as qualifying for May 24 primary wraps up|last=Cason|first=Mike|work=AL.com|date=January 28, 2022|access-date=February 2, 2022}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.al.com/news/2022/03/alabama-democrats-remove-trump-supporter-from-primary-ballot.html|title=Alabama Democrats remove Trump-supporter from primary ballot|last=Gattis|first=Paul|work=AL.com|date=March 4, 2022|access-date=March 5, 2022}}</ref> in the Democratic primary. Britt won the Senate election, becoming the first woman elected to the United States Senate from Alabama.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Shapero |first=Julia |date=2022-11-09 |title=Republican Katie Britt first woman elected to Senate in Alabama |url=https://thehill.com/homenews/campaign/3727164-republican-katie-britt-first-woman-elected-to-senate-in-alabama/ |access-date=2022-11-09 |website=The Hill |language=en-US}}</ref>

{{See also|List of United States senators from Alaska|2022 United States House of Representatives election in Alaska}}
Three-term Republican [[Lisa Murkowski]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Alaska|re-elected in 2016]] with 44.4% of the vote. Alaska voters passed a [[2020 Alaska Measure 2|ballot initiative]] in 2020 that adopted a new [[Top-four primary|top-four ranked-choice voting]] system: all candidates compete in a [[nonpartisan blanket primary]], the top four candidates advance to the general election, and the winner is determined by [[instant-runoff voting]] using [[Ranked-choice voting in the United States|ranked-choice ballots]]. On March 30, following the Alaska Republican Party's decision to censure senator Murkowski, former [[Alaska Department of Administration]] commissioner Kelly Tshibaka announced her campaign against Murkowski, later receiving [[Donald Trump]]'s endorsement.<ref>{{cite news |last1=Rogers |first1=Alex |title=Republican Kelly Tshibaka launches Senate campaign against Lisa Murkowski |url=https://www.cnn.com/2021/03/29/politics/lisa-murkowski-gop-challenger-tshibaka/index.html |work=CNN |access-date=March 31, 2021|date=March 30, 2021}}</ref> Republican [[Governor of Alaska|governor]] [[Mike Dunleavy (politician)|Mike Dunleavy]], who was considered another potential challenger to Murkowski, instead [[2022 Alaska gubernatorial election|ran for re-election]].<ref>{{cite web|last=Brooks|first=James|date=August 13, 2021|title=Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy will run for re-election in 2022|url=https://www.adn.com/politics/2021/08/13/alaska-gov-mike-dunleavy-files-paperwork-to-begin-fundraising-for-re-election-in-2022/|access-date=August 14, 2021|website=[[Anchorage Daily News]]|language=en-US}}</ref>

Murkowski, Tshibaka, Republican Buzz Kelley, and Democrat Pat Chesbro advanced to the general election.<ref>{{cite news |title=Murkowski and Trump-backed Tshibaka advance to Alaska general election, NBC projects |work=[[CNBC]] |date=August 17, 2022 |access-date=September 15, 2022 |first=Kevin |last=Breuninger |url=https://www.cnbc.com/2022/08/17/murkowski-and-trump-backed-tshibaka-advance-to-alaska-general-election-nbc-projects.html}}</ref> Kelley suspended his campaign in September and endorsed Tshibaka, although his name remained on the ballot.<ref>{{cite news |title=Alaska US Senate hopeful drops bid, backs fellow Republican |first=Mark |last=Thiessen |date=September 12, 2022 |access-date=September 15, 2022 |work=[[Associated Press]] |url=https://apnews.com/article/2022-midterm-elections-sarah-palin-donald-trump-general-campaigns-8b1b8dc64c4b2022e63f39e3fd359249}}</ref>

Murkowski received a slight plurality of the first-choice votes and a majority of all votes following the ranked choice tabulation, winning re-election to a fourth full term.<ref name=NYTIMES />

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Arizona}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from Arizona|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Arizona}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Mark Kelly]] took office on December 2, 2020, after winning a [[2020 United States Senate special election in Arizona|special election]] with 51.2% of the vote.

Six-term senator and 2008 Republican presidential nominee [[John McCain]] was re-elected to this seat in 2016. He died on August 25, 2018, and former U.S. senator [[Jon Kyl]] was appointed to replace him. Kyl resigned at the end of 2018 and U.S. representative [[Martha McSally]] was appointed to replace him. Kelly defeated McSally in the 2020 special election.

In the Republican primary, [[Blake Masters]], the chairman of the Thiel Foundation, defeated Jim Lamon, chair of the solar power company Depcom,<ref name=Lamon2022>{{cite web |last1=Sanchez |first1=Yvonne |title=Jim Lamon is the 1st Republican to enter Arizona's 2022 Senate race |url=https://www.azcentral.com/story/news/politics/elections/2021/05/03/jim-lamon-first-republican-enter-arizonas-2022-senate-race/4928025001/ |website=azcentral |access-date=May 3, 2021|date=May 3, 2021}}</ref> and [[Arizona Attorney General|Arizona attorney general]] [[Mark Brnovich]].<ref>{{cite news|title=Arizona Attorney General Mark Brnovich launches US Senate campaign|work=Arizona Central|url=https://www.azcentral.com/story/news/politics/elections/2021/06/10/mark-brnovich-launches-us-senate-campaign/7644924002/|author=Wingett Sanchez, Yvonne|date=June 10, 2021}}</ref>

Kelly defeated Masters, winning election to his first full term.<ref name=NYTIMES />

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Arkansas}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from Arkansas|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Arkansas}}
Two-term Republican [[John Boozman]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Arkansas|re-elected in 2016]] with 59.8% of the vote. Boozman ran for a third term.<ref name="auto11">{{cite news |title=Senator Boozman seeks re-election in 2022 |url=https://www.swtimes.com/story/news/2021/03/15/senator-boozman-announces-re-election-bid-2022/4710195001/ |access-date=March 17, 2021 |agency=Southwest Times Record |date=March 15, 2021}}</ref>

Boozman defeated former NFL player and U.S. Army veteran [[Jake Bequette]],<ref name="auto10">{{cite news |title=Former NFL player, Iraq war veteran Jake Bequette challenges Arkansas Sen. Boozman|url=https://www.foxnews.com/politics/former-nfl-player-iraq-war-veteran-bequette-challenges-arkansas-sen-boozman|access-date=July 12, 2021 |agency=Fox News|date=July 12, 2021|author=Steinhauser, Paul}}</ref> gun range owner and [[2018 Arkansas gubernatorial election|2018]] gubernatorial candidate Jan Morgan,<ref>{{cite news |title=Former gubernatorial candidate Jan Morgan launches U.S. Senate bid |url=https://www.arkansasonline.com/news/2021/mar/17/former-gubernatorial-candidate-launches-bid-us-sen/ |access-date=March 18, 2021 |agency=Arkansas Democrat Gazette |date=March 17, 2021|author=Lockwood, Frank E.}}</ref> and pastor Heath Loftis<ref name="auto6">{{cite web |last1=Brantley |first1=Max |title=Another challenger to John Boozman from the right |url=https://arktimes.com/arkansas-blog/2021/03/24/another-challenger-to-john-boozman-from-the-right |publisher=Arkansas Times |access-date=April 9, 2021 |date=March 24, 2021}}</ref> in the Republican primary.<ref>{{Cite web |date=2022-05-24 |title=Sen. John Boozman clinches Republican nomination in Arkansas |url=https://www.pbs.org/newshour/politics/sen-john-boozman-clinches-republican-nomination-in-arkansas |access-date=2022-06-30 |website=PBS NewsHour |language=en-us}}</ref> A fourth challenger, corporate analyst Michael Deel withdrew prior to the primary election citing a lack of viability.<ref>{{cite news |last1=Bowden |first1=Bill |title=Gay Republican sets eye on U.S. Senate seat held by Boozman to "make difference" |url=https://www.arkansasonline.com/news/2021/may/09/gay-republican-sets-eye-on-us-senate-to-make/ |publisher=Arkansas Democrat Gazette |date=May 9, 2021 |access-date=May 15, 2021}}</ref>

Natalie James, a real estate broker from [[Little Rock]],<ref name="auto5">{{cite news |date=January 13, 2021 |title=Dan Whitfield to run against Boozman for US Senate seat in 2022 |work=KFSM|url=https://www.5newsonline.com/article/news/politics/dan-whitfield-to-run-against-boozman-for-us-senate-seat-in-2022/527-bc399d1f-1203-4ab2-9060-2a7b34fdf99a |access-date=January 13, 2021|author=Schmidt, Haleigh}}</ref> defeated Dan Whitfield, who attempted to run as an independent for Arkansas' other U.S. Senate seat in 2020 but failed to meet the ballot access requirements,<ref>{{cite news |date=October 1, 2020 |title=Dan Whitfield suspends US Senate race after not getting on the ballot |work=KFSM|url=https://www.5newsonline.com/article/news/politics/dan-whitfield-suspends-us-senate-race-after-not-getting-on-the-ballot/527-dc966fde-0d1a-4b1b-90b3-ad279b60fcc8 |access-date=January 13, 2021|author=Gilker, Kathryn}}</ref> and former [[Pine Bluff, Arkansas|Pine Bluff]] [[City alderman]] Jack Foster in the Democratic primary.<ref name="auto9">{{cite news |last1=Bailey |first1=Austin |title=Arkansas Sen. John Boozman gets another challenger, this time from the left |url=https://arktimes.com/arkansas-blog/2021/04/09/arkansas-sen-john-boozman-gets-another-challenger-this-time-from-the-left |access-date=April 9, 2021 |agency=Arkansas Times |date=April 9, 2021}}</ref>

Boozman defeated James, winning re-election to a third term.<ref name=NYTIMES />

{{Main|2022 United States Senate elections in California}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from California|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in California}}
Incumbent Democrat [[Alex Padilla]] took office on January 20, 2021. He was appointed by Governor [[Gavin Newsom]] following the resignation of incumbent Democrat [[Kamala Harris]] on January 18, 2021, in advance of her swearing-in as [[Vice President of the United States]].<ref>{{Cite news |date=December 22, 2020 |title=Alex Padilla Will Replace Kamala Harris in the Senate |work=The New York Times |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2020/12/22/us/politics/alex-padilla-kamala-california-senate.html|author=Hubler, Shawn}}</ref>

Due to a rule change, there were two ballot items for the same seat: a general election, to elect a [[Classes of United States senators|Class 3 senator]] to a full term beginning with the [[118th United States Congress]], sworn in on January 3, 2023, and a special election, to fill that seat for the final weeks of the [[117th United States Congress|117th Congress]]. Padilla ran in both races,<ref name="CA">{{Cite news |last=Wilson |first=Reid |date=September 28, 2021 |title=California rule change means Padilla faces extra election |work=[[The Hill (newspaper)|The Hill]] |url=https://thehill.com/homenews/campaign/574242-california-rule-change-means-padilla-faces-extra-election |url-status=live |access-date=September 28, 2021 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210928225230/https://thehill.com/homenews/campaign/574242-california-rule-change-means-padilla-faces-extra-election |archive-date=September 28, 2021}}</ref> as did the Republican nominee, attorney Mark Meuser. With his advancement out of the primary, Mark P. Meuser ({{IPAc-en|ˈ|m|ɔɪ|ʒ|ər}} {{respell|MOY|zhər}}) became the first Republican since [[2012 United States Senate election in California|2012]] to advance to the general election, as both the [[2016 United States Senate election in California|2016]] and [[2018 United States Senate election in California|2018 Senate elections]] solely featured Democrats as the top two candidates. This race was a rematch between the two, as both had previously run for the secretary of state in [[2018 California Secretary of State election|2018]]. Padilla defeated Meuser in both races, winning election to his first full term.

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Colorado}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from Colorado|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Colorado}}
Two-term Democrat [[Michael Bennet]] took office on January 21, 2009, after being appointed by then [[Colorado Governor|Colorado governor]] [[Bill Ritter]] to replace outgoing Democrat [[Ken Salazar]], who was nominated by then president [[Barack Obama]] to serve as [[United States Secretary of the Interior|United States secretary of the interior]]. He had narrowly won reelection bids, in [[2010 United States Senate election in Colorado|2010]] to his first full term, with 48.08% of the vote, and, in [[2016 United States Senate election in Colorado|2016]] to his second, with 49.97% of the vote.

In the Republican primary, construction company owner Joe O'Dea defeated [[Colorado House of Representatives|state representative]] [[Ron Hanks]].<ref>{{cite web |last1=Fish |first1=Sandra |last2=Paul |first2=Jesse |title=Ron Hanks files to run for U.S. Senate in Colorado |date=October 2021 |url=https://coloradosun.com/2021/10/01/ron-hanks-announces-senate-bid/ |publisher=[[The Colorado Sun]] |access-date=October 1, 2021}}</ref><ref>{{cite web | url=https://gazette.com/elections/former-colorado-gov-bill-owens-endorses-joe-odea-in-us-senate-primary/article_7e61b229-2e3c-550d-88fd-68772bfa3f25.html | title=Former Colorado Gov. Bill Owens endorses Joe O'Dea in US Senate primary|author=Luning, Ernest|date=May 11, 2022|website=Gazette.com}}</ref>

Bennet defeated O'Dea, winning election to his third full term.

{{See also|List of United States senators from Florida|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Florida}}
Two-term Republican [[Marco Rubio]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Florida|re-elected in 2016]] with 52% of the vote. He announced on November 9, 2020, via Facebook, that he was running for re-election.<ref>{{cite web |title=Marco Rubio 2022 US Senate |url=https://secure.winred.com/marco-rubio-for-senate/09112020-join-me/ |website=WinRed}}</ref>

U.S. representative [[Val Demings]] was the Democratic nominee.<ref name=DemingsRunning>{{cite web|last=Greenwood|first=Max|date=June 9, 2021|title=Florida Rep. Val Demings officially enters Senate race against Rubio|url=https://thehill.com/homenews/campaign/557485-florida-rep-val-demings-officially-enters-senate-race-against-rubio|access-date=June 9, 2021|website=[[The Hill (newspaper)|The Hill]]|language=en}}</ref>

[[Ivanka Trump]], daughter and former [[Senior Advisor to the President of the United States|senior advisor]] to former president [[Donald Trump]], was seen as a potential candidate to challenge Rubio for the Republican nomination.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/news/2021/01/15/ivanka-trump-political-future-459758|title=Ivanka's political future comes into sharper focus|website=Politico|date=January 15, 2021|author1=McGraw, Meredith|author2=Caputo, Marc|author3=Stein, Sam}}</ref> However, on February 18, 2021, it was confirmed that she would not seek the nomination.<ref>{{cite news|url=https://www.politico.com/news/2021/02/18/ivanka-trump-marco-rubio-senate-bid-469774|title=Ivanka Trump passes up Senate bid against Rubio|website=Politico|date=February 18, 2021|author=Desiderio, Andrew}}</ref>

Rubio defeated Demings, winning re-election to a third term.

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Georgia}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from Georgia|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Georgia}}

Incumbent Democrat [[Raphael Warnock]] won the [[2020–21 United States Senate special election in Georgia|2020–2021 special election]] against incumbent Republican [[Kelly Loeffler]] to fill the remainder of former senator [[Johnny Isakson]]'s term. Isakson resigned at the end of 2019 due to health problems and eventually died less than two years later, in 2021. Loeffler was appointed by Governor [[Brian Kemp]] following Isakson's resignation. No candidate in the open election on November 3 received the 50% required by Georgia law to avoid a run-off, a type of election colloquially known as a "[[jungle primary]]"<ref>{{cite news |last= Keenan|first= Sean|date= March 20, 2020|title= What in the world is a jungle primary, and what's in store for Georgia's? |url= https://www.atlantamagazine.com/news-culture-articles/what-in-the-world-is-a-jungle-primary-and-whats-in-store-for-georgias/|work=[[Atlanta (magazine)|Atlanta]]|location= |access-date= February 16, 2021}}</ref>—Warnock received just 32.9% of the vote—and so, a run-off election between Warnock and Loeffler was held on January 5, 2021, which Warnock won with 51% of the vote.

Former Republican senator [[David Perdue]], who narrowly lost his race to Democratic challenger [[Jon Ossoff]] in 2021,<ref>{{cite news |last= Bluestein|first= Greg|date= February 15, 2021|title= Perdue explores Senate comeback bid against Warnock in 2022 |url= https://www.ajc.com/politics/politics-blog/perdue-explores-senate-comeback-bid-against-warnock-in-2022/ZPYGUCAUIFCKRODBPA2PS4CCDE/|work= [[The Atlanta Journal-Constitution]] |location= |access-date= February 15, 2021}}</ref> and former U.S. representative [[Doug Collins (politician)|Doug Collins]]<ref>{{cite web |last1=Arkin |first1=James |title=Doug Collins says he won't run for Georgia Senate, governor |url=https://www.politico.com/news/2021/04/26/doug-collins-georgia-senate-governor-race-484601 |website=Politico |access-date=April 26, 2021|date=April 26, 2021}}</ref> both considered challenging Warnock, but eventually announced they were not running.<ref>{{cite news |last= Arkin|first= James|date= February 23, 2021|title= Perdue rules out Georgia Senate comeback in 2022 |url= https://www.politico.com/news/2021/02/23/perdue-georgia-senate-2022-471131/|work= [[Politico]] |location= |access-date= February 23, 2021}}</ref>

Former [[NFL]] player [[Herschel Walker]],<ref>{{cite web |last1=Machlin |first1=Tzvi |title=Legendary NFL Running Back Considering U.S. Senate Run |url=https://thespun.com/sec/georgia-bulldogs/legendary-nfl-running-back-considering-u-s-senate-run |website=College Spun |date=April 11, 2021 |publisher=The Spun |access-date=April 11, 2021}}</ref> who had been endorsed by former president Donald Trump,<ref>{{cite news |last1=Rogers |first1=Alex |title=With Trump's backing, Walker freezes Senate GOP field in Georgia |url=https://www.cnn.com/2021/04/22/politics/georgia-senate-race-2022-herschel-walker-donald-trump/index.html |website=CNN |access-date=April 22, 2021|author2=Raju, Manu|date=April 22, 2021}}</ref> defeated banking executive Latham Saddler<ref>{{cite web |last1=Bluestein |first1=Greg |title=Who would challenge Raphael Warnock in 2022 |url=https://www.ajc.com/politics/politics-blog/who-could-challenge-raphael-warnock-in-2022/ZPXXNMFLLNAEBCVD7QAX65CUJA/ |website=The Atlanta Journal-Constitution |access-date=April 7, 2021|date=April 26, 2021}}</ref> and others in the Republican primary.

In the general election, no candidate received a majority of the vote.<ref>{{Cite news |last=Dorn |first=Sara |date=November 9, 2022 |title=Walker, Warnock Headed For A Runoff In Georgia Senate Race |work=[[Forbes]] |url=https://www.forbes.com/sites/saradorn/2022/11/09/walker-warnock-headed-for-a-runoff-in-georgia-senate-race/ |url-status=live |access-date=November 11, 2022 |archive-url=https://archive.today/20221111234703/https://www.forbes.com/sites/saradorn/2022/11/09/walker-warnock-headed-for-a-runoff-in-georgia-senate-race/?sh=6d571faf2d7b |archive-date=November 11, 2022}}</ref> Warnock defeated Walker in a [[Two-round system|runoff]] between the top-two finishers on December 6.<ref>{{Cite web |last1=Barrow |first1=Bill |last2=Amy |first2=Jeff |date=2022-12-06 |title=Democratic Sen. Warnock wins Georgia runoff against Walker |url=https://apnews.com/article/2022-midterm-elections-walker-warnock-runoff-3d4e4d1ab1760792454e1cbd618ce332 |access-date=2022-12-07 |website=[[AP News]] |language=en |place=[[Atlanta]]}}</ref>

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Hawaii}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from Hawaii|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Hawaii}}
One-term Democrat [[Brian Schatz]] was appointed to the Senate in 2012, following the death of incumbent [[Daniel Inouye]]. He won a [[2014 United States Senate special election in Hawaii|special election]] to finish Inouye's term in 2014, and won his first full term in [[2016 United States Senate election in Hawaii|2016]] with 73.6% of the vote. Republican [[Hawaii House of Representatives|state representative]] [[Bob McDermott]] challenged Schatz.<ref name="Hawaii News Now">{{cite web |title=Republican state representative announces run against Schatz for US Senate |url=https://www.hawaiinewsnow.com/2022/01/18/republican-state-representative-announces-run-against-schatz-us-senate/ |website=www.hawaiinewsnow.com |publisher=[[Hawaii News Now]] |date=January 18, 2022 |access-date=January 19, 2022}}</ref>

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Indiana}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from Indiana|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Indiana}}
First-term Republican [[Todd Young]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Indiana|elected in 2016]] with 52.1% of the vote. He announced on March 2, 2021, that he was running for re-election.<ref name="auto8">{{cite web|url=https://www.wane.com/news/your-local-election-hq/im-running-todd-young-to-seek-re-election-to-us-senate/|title='I'm running': Todd Young to seek re-election to US Senate|date=March 2, 2021|access-date=March 2, 2021|website=wane.com|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210419214118/https://www.wane.com/news/your-local-election-hq/im-running-todd-young-to-seek-re-election-to-us-senate/|archive-date=April 19, 2021|url-status=dead}}</ref> [[Hammond, Indiana|Hammond]] mayor [[Thomas McDermott Jr.]] won the Democratic nomination.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.indystar.com/story/news/politics/2021/08/18/indiana-senate-hammond-mayor-files-sen-young-seat/8186048002/|title=Hammond Mayor Thomas McDermott Jr. files to run for Sen. Todd Young's seat in 2022|first=Kaitlin|last=Lange|website=The Indianapolis Star|date=August 18, 2021}}</ref> James Sceniak, a behavior therapist, was the Libertarian candidate.

Young defeated McDermott, winning re-election to a second term.

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Iowa}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from Iowa|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Iowa}}
Seven-term Republican [[Chuck Grassley]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Iowa|re-elected in 2016]] with 60.1% of the vote. He sought re-election to an eighth term.<ref name=GrassleyRunning>{{cite web|date=September 24, 2021|title=Grassley will seek reelection, boosting GOP's majority hopes|url=https://www.politico.com/news/2021/09/24/chuck-grassley-2022-elections-iowa-514090|access-date=September 24, 2021|website=Politico|language=en|author=Everett, Burgess}}</ref>

In the Republican primary, Grassley defeated state senator [[Jim Carlin (politician)|Jim Carlin]].<ref name="Hall">{{cite web|last=Hall|first=Jacob|date=February 12, 2021|title=BREAKING: State Sen. Jim Carlin is running for United States Senate seat currently held by Chuck Grassley, says if our votes do not count, we no longer have a representative government|url=https://theiowastandard.com/breaking-state-sen-jim-carlin-is-running-for-united-states-senate-seat-currently-held-by-chuck-grassley/|access-date=February 16, 2021|website=The Iowa Standard}}</ref>

Retired [[Admiral (United States)|admiral]] and former aide to U.S. senator [[Ted Kennedy]], [[Michael Franken]],<ref>{{Cite news |last=Belin |first=Laura |date=February 20, 2020 |title=Chuck Grassley says grandson's "never expressed" interest in U.S. Senate bid |work=Bleeding Heartland |url=https://www.bleedingheartland.com/2020/02/20/chuck-grassley-says-grandsons-never-expressed-interest-in-u-s-senate-bid/ |access-date=September 14, 2020}}</ref> was the Democratic nominee. Franken defeated former U.S. representative [[Abby Finkenauer]] in the primary in what was seen as a major [[Upset (competition)|upset]].<ref>{{cite web |date=June 8, 2022 |title=Michael Franken to challenge Chuck Grassley in November 2022 election |url=https://www.desmoinesregister.com/story/news/politics/2022/06/07/iowa-election-results-democrat-running-against-chuck-grassley-november/7488982001/ |accessdate=June 8, 2022 |publisher=Desmoinesregister.com|author=Akin, Katie}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web |last=Pfannenstiel |first=Brianne |title=Mike Franken wins Democratic US Senate primary; to take on Chuck Grassley in November |url=https://www.desmoinesregister.com/story/news/politics/2022/06/07/mike-franken-iowa-senate-democratic-primary-election-results-face-chuck-grassley/9909974002/ |access-date=2022-08-21 |website=The Des Moines Register |language=en-US}}</ref>

Grassley defeated Franken, winning re-election to an eighth term.

== Kansas ==
{{Infobox election

{{See also|List of United States senators from Kansas|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Kansas}}
Two-term Republican [[Jerry Moran]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Kansas|re-elected in 2016]] with 62.2% of the vote. He announced that he was seeking re-election in 2020.<ref name="auto1">{{Cite news |date=November 11, 2020 |title=Sen. Jerry Moran will seek reelection in 2022, an aide said |agency=Associated Press |work=KSHB 41 Kansas City |url=https://www.kshb.com/news/election-2020/sen-jerry-moran-will-seek-reelection-in-2020-an-aide-said |access-date=November 21, 2020}}</ref> Democratic [[United Methodist]] pastor and former [[Kansas City, Kansas|Kansas City]] mayor [[Mark Holland (American politician)|Mark Holland]] challenged Moran.<ref name="KSHB">{{cite news |last1=Kaut |first1=Steve |title=Former KCK Mayor Mark Holland to run for US Senate seat in Kansas |url=https://www.kshb.com/news/local-news/former-kck-mayor-mark-holland-to-run-for-us-senate-seat-in-kansas |publisher=KSHB |date=November 11, 2021 |access-date=November 13, 2021}}</ref>

Two-term Republican [[Rand Paul]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Kentucky|re-elected in 2016]] with 57.3% of the vote. He sought re-election to a third term.<ref name="auto3">{{cite web |last1=Dean |first1=Kelly |title=Sen. Rand Paul discusses upcoming plans for re-election 2022 |url=https://www.wbko.com/2021/02/25/sen-rand-paul-discusses-upcoming-plans-for-re-election-2022/ |website=[[ABC News (United States)|ABC News]] |access-date=March 6, 2021|date=February 25, 2021}}</ref>

Former Democratic [[Kentucky House of Representatives|state representative]] and 2020 runner-up in the Democratic U.S. Senate primary [[Charles Booker (American politician)|Charles Booker]] won the Democratic primary.<ref>{{cite web|last=Watkins|first=Morgan|title=Rand Paul and Charles Booker nab US Senate primary wins and will face off in November|url=https://www.courier-journal.com/story/news/politics/2022/05/17/us-senate-rand-paul-charles-booker-win-democrat-gop-nominations-kentucky-primary/9676003002/|website=Louisville Courier Journal|date=May 17, 2022|access-date=August 4, 2022}}</ref>

One-term Democrat [[Chris Van Hollen]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Maryland|elected in 2016]] with 60.9% of the vote, and ran for a second term.<ref name=VanHollenRunning>{{cite web|last=Kurtz|first=Josh|date=April 16, 2021|title=Mizeur Collects $350K for Congressional Bid; Aruna Miller Raising Money for Possible House Run|url=https://www.marylandmatters.org/2021/04/16/mizeur-collects-350k-for-congressional-bid-aruna-miller-raising-money-for-possible-house-run/|access-date=May 7, 2021|website=Maryland Matters|language=en-US}}</ref>

Despite previously indicating that he had no interest in pursuing the Republican nomination for the U.S. Senate, incumbent [[Governor of Maryland|governor]] [[Larry Hogan]], who was term-limited and scheduled to leave office in 2023, told conservative talk radio host [[Hugh Hewitt]] in October 2021 that he was considering challenging Van Hollen. Hogan ultimately decided not to challenge Van Hollen on February 8, 2022.<ref>{{cite news|url=https://www.nytimes.com/2020/07/07/us/politics/larry-hogan-maryland-book-tour-2024-campaign.html| title= Hogan, Maryland Governor, Plans Book Tour as He Eyes 2024 White House Run| newspaper= The New York Times|date=July 7, 2020| last1= Broadwater| first1= Luke}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|title=Larry Hogan ponders Maryland Senate race|url=https://www.yahoo.com/now/larry-hogan-ponders-maryland-senate-140900443.html|access-date=2021-11-02|website=Yahoo|language=en-US|author=Drucker, David M.|date=October 8, 2021}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|date=2022-02-08|title=AP source: GOP Maryland Gov. Hogan won't run for Senate|url=https://www.wbaltv.com/article/maryland-governor-larry-hogan-wont-run-for-senate/39014734|access-date=2022-02-08|website=WBAL|language=en}}</ref>

Van Hollen and construction company owner Chris Chaffee won their respective primaries on July 19.<ref>{{cite news|url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/elections/election-results/maryland/2022-primaries/|title=2022 Maryland primary elections results|newspaper=[[The Washington Post]]|date=July 19, 2022|access-date=July 20, 2022}}</ref>

{{See also|List of United States senators from Missouri|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Missouri}}
Two-term Republican [[Roy Blunt]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Missouri|re-elected in 2016]] with 49.2% of the vote. He did not seek re-election.<ref name="auto"/>

State attorney general [[Eric Schmitt]] defeated former governor [[Eric Greitens]]<ref>{{Cite news |last=Willeke |first=Becky |date=June 10, 2020 |title=Former Missouri Gov. Eric Greitens may be looking at running for office |work=[[KTVI]] |url=https://fox2now.com/news/former-missouri-gov-eric-greitens-files-to-run-for-office-in-2024/ |access-date=October 25, 2020}}</ref> and U.S. representatives [[Vicky Hartzler]] and [[Billy Long]] in the Republican primary.<ref name="Hartzler">{{cite web |last=Suntrup |first=Jack |date=June 10, 2021 |title=Vicky Hartzler, GOP congresswoman from western Missouri, running for U.S. Senate |url= https://www.stltoday.com/news/local/govt-and-politics/vicky-hartzler-gop-congresswoman-from-western-missouri-running-for-u-s-senate/article_48b96cf9-ee0f-51a3-b77c-25dc805a227c.html|access-date=June 10, 2021|website=STL Today}}</ref><ref name=MOprimaryRes>{{cite web|last=Suntrup|first=Jack|title=Eric Schmitt and Trudy Busch Valentine win Missouri's Senate primary contests|url=https://www.stltoday.com/news/local/govt-and-politics/eric-schmitt-and-trudy-busch-valentine-win-missouri-s-senate-primary-contests/article_f2a3d783-0d6c-50ec-abe6-e2566fe8c114.html|website=St. Louis Post-Dispatch|date=August 2, 2022|access-date=August 4, 2022}}</ref><ref name="auto16">{{cite web|last=Bowman|first=Bridget|date=August 3, 2021|title=GOP Rep. Smith|Kevin Smith]], and others in the Republican primary.<ref name=NH2022/>

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in New York}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from New York|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in New York}}Incumbent four-term [[Democratic Party (United States)|Democratic Party]] senator [[Chuck Schumer]], who had served as [[Party leaders of the United States Senate|Senate majority leader]] since 2021 and had held New York's [[Classes of United States senators|Class 3]] Senate seat since defeating [[Republican Party (United States)|Republican Party]] incumbent [[Al D'Amato]] in [[1998 United States Senate election in New York|1998]], ran for a fifth term. Republican Party challenger [[Joe Pinion]] was the first black Senate nominee of any major party in New York history. The filing deadline for the June primary was April 7, 2022.<ref name="aoc-cillizza">{{Cite web |last=Cillizza |first=Chris |author-link=Chris Cillizza |date=April 20, 2020 |title=Does Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez have her eye on a Senate seat? |url=https://www.cnn.com/2020/04/20/politics/aoc-2022-senate-schumer/index.html |website=[[CNN Politics]]}}</ref> Schumer became the longest-serving U.S. senator in the state's history once his fifth term began.<ref>{{cite news |last1=Matthews |first1=Karen |last2=Hajela |first2=Deepti |title=New York's Schumer keeps Senate seat, but majority role up for grabs |url=https://www.timesofisrael.com/new-yorks-schumer-keeps-senate-seat-but-majority-role-up-for-grabs/ |access-date=2022-11-09 |website=[[The Times of Israel]] |language=en-US |issn=0040-7909}}</ref>

Though Schumer was comfortably re-elected, he lost significant support on [[Long Island]] and [[Upstate New York]] compared to [[2016 United States Senate election in New York|his last election in 2016]]. Pinion flipped the more conservative counties that Schumer had won in his previous runs, as well as some Democratic leaning counties such as [[Nassau County, New York|Nassau]], [[Saratoga County, New York|Saratoga]], [[Broome County, New York|Broome]], [[Clinton County, New York|Clinton]], and [[Essex County, New York|Essex]]. However, Schumer's lead was large enough in [[Politics of New York City|New York City]] that it was called by most media outlets the moment the polls closed.<ref>{{Cite news |last=McKinley |first=Jesse |date=2022-11-09 |title=Tough N.Y. Election Holds Lessons for Republicans and Democrats Alike |language=en-US |work=The New York Times |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2022/11/09/nyregion/ny-election-takeaways.html |access-date=2022-11-12 |issn=0362-4331}}</ref>

Despite Democrats overperforming expectations on a national level during this cycle, this race was the most competitive in Schumer's Senate career since his first election in [[1998 United States Senate election in New York|1998]], when he won by 10.5%.

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in North Carolina}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from North Carolina|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in North Carolina}}
Three-term Republican [[Richard Burr]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in North Carolina|re-elected in 2016]] with 51.0% of the vote. Burr pledged to retire in 2023.<ref name="ccampbell1"/>
 
Veteran and senior fellow at the Center for International Policy, Mathew Hoh ran for senate with the [[Green Party of the United States|Green Party]].<ref name=NC2022>{{cite web |title=Candidate Lists |url=https://www.ncsbe.gov/results-data/candidate-lists#current |date=February 24, 2022}}</ref>

[[Lara Trump]], daughter-in-law of former president [[Donald Trump]], and [[Lieutenant Governor of North Carolina|Lieutenant Governor]] [[Mark Robinson (American politician)|Mark Robinson]] briefly considered running for U.S. Senate, but both decided not to run.<ref name="LTrump1">{{cite news |last=Karni |first=Annie |date=November 19, 2020 |title=Will Lara Trump Be the Next Trump on a Ballot? |url=https://www.nytimes.com/2020/11/19/us/politics/lara-trump.html |access-date=November 19, 2020 |website=The New York Times}}</ref><ref>{{cite news |last1=Choi |first1=Matthew |last2=Isenstadt |first2=Alex |last3=Arkin |first3=James |date=November 19, 2020 |title=Lara Trump considers run for Senate in North Carolina |work=[[Politico]] |url=https://www.politico.com/news/2020/11/19/lara-trump-north-carolina-senate-seat-438447 |access-date=November 22, 2020}}</ref><ref name="auto17"/><ref>{{cite web |title=NC Lt. Governor Mark Robinson Won't Run for Senate In 2022 |date=April 20, 2021 |url=https://www.wunc.org/politics/2021-04-20/north-carolina-lt-gov-mark-robinson-wont-run-senate-2022 |publisher=WUNC |access-date=April 20, 2021}}</ref>

U.S. representative [[Ted Budd]], who was endorsed by former president [[Donald Trump]], easily defeated former [[Governor of North Carolina|governor]] [[Pat McCrory]] in the Republican primary.<ref>{{cite news|last=Murphy|first=Brian|author2=Théoden Janes|date=April 14, 2021|url=https://www.charlotteobserver.com/news/politics-government/article250602039.html|title=Former North Carolina Gov. Pat McCrory announces bid for US Senate seat in 2022|work=The Charlotte Observer}}</ref><ref name="auto12">{{cite web|title= Ted Budd launches Senate bid in North Carolina|url= https://www.politico.com/news/2021/04/28/ted-budd-senate-race-north-carolina-484868|access-date=April 28, 2021|website=Politico|date=April 28, 2021|author=Arkin, James}}</ref><ref name="auto17">{{cite web|last=Polus|first=Sarah|date=June 5, 2021|title=Lara Trump on Senate bid: 'No for now, not no forever'|url=https://thehill.com/homenews/news/557022-lara-trump-on-senate-bid-no-for-now-not-no-forever|access-date=June 6, 2021|website=The Hill|language=en}}</ref>

Former [[Chief Justice|chief justice]] of the [[North Carolina Supreme Court|state Supreme Court]] [[Cheri Beasley]]<ref name="axelrod1">{{cite web|last=Axelrod|first=Tal|title=Former North Carolina chief justice launches Senate campaign|url=https://thehill.com/homenews/campaign/550513-former-north-carolina-chief-justice-launches-senate-campaign|access-date=April 27, 2021|website=The Hill|date=April 27, 2021}}</ref> easily defeated [[Beaufort, North Carolina|Beaufort]] mayor Rett Newton<ref>{{cite web |last1=Murphey |first1=Brian |title=Rett Newton: Beaufort mayor joins NC's US Senate race |url=https://www.newsobserver.com/news/politics-government/article250463631.html |website=The News & Observer |access-date=April 7, 2021}}</ref> in the Democratic primary.<ref>{{cite web |last=Murphy |first=Brian |title=Could Lara Trump run in NC in 2022? Open Senate seat expected to draw a GOP crowd |url=https://www.newsobserver.com/news/politics-government/article247295799.html |access-date=November 20, 2020 |website=The News & Observer}}</ref>

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in North Dakota}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from North Dakota|2022 United States House of Representatives election in North Dakota}}
Two-term Republican [[John Hoeven]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in North Dakota|re-elected in 2016]] with 78.5% of the vote. On February 4, 2021, Hoeven campaign spokesman Dan Larson indicated that Hoeven was running for re-election in 2022.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.valleynewslive.com/2021/02/05/senator-john-hoeven-seeking-3rd-term/|title=Senator John Hoeven Seeking 3rd Term|first=Cordell|last=Wagner|website=Valley News Live|date=February 5, 2021}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.kvrr.com/2021/02/04/hoeven-to-seek-third-term-in-senate/|title=Hoeven to seek third term in Senate|date=February 4, 2021|author=Monk, Jim|work=KVRR}}</ref> [[University of Jamestown]] engineering professor Katrina Christiansen defeated businessman Michael Steele in the Democratic primary election.<ref name = "two democrats">{{Cite web|url=https://www.inforum.com/news/north-dakota/jamestown-professor-to-run-for-us-senate-as-democrat|title = Two North Dakota Democrats enter US Senate race|date = February 14, 2022|author=Turley, Jeremy|work=inforum.com}}</ref> Former [[North Dakota House of Representatives|state representative]] [[Rick Becker]] challenged Hoeven in the Republican primary but withdrew after losing the convention.<ref name=Endorsed>{{cite web |last1=Turley |first1=Jeremy |title=Hoeven beats Becker for North Dakota GOP endorsement in Senate race |url=https://www.inforum.com/news/north-dakota/hoeven-beats-becker-for-north-dakota-gop-endorsement-in-senate-race |website=www.inforum.com |date=April 2, 2022 |access-date=April 3, 2022}}</ref>

Hoeven and Christiansen won their respective primaries on June 14.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/2022-election/results/north-dakota/|website=Politico|title=2022 North Dakota primary results|date=June 14, 2022|access-date=June 14, 2022}}</ref>

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Ohio}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from Ohio|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Ohio}}
Two-term Republican [[Rob Portman]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Ohio|re-elected in 2016]] with 58% of the vote. On January 25, 2021, he announced that he would not be running for re-election.<ref name="Williams"/>

[[Venture capitalist]] and author [[JD Vance]] was nominated in a crowded and competitive Republican primary, defeating [[United States Marine Corps Reserve|USMCR]] veteran and former [[Ohio State Treasurer|state treasurer]] [[Josh Mandel]], [[Ohio Senate|state senator]] [[Matt Dolan]], investment banker Mike Gibbons, and former [[Ohio Republican Party]] chair [[Jane Timken]], among others.<ref>{{cite web|title=JD Vance jumps into Ohio Senate primary|date=July 1, 2021|website=The Hill|url=https://thehill.com/homenews/campaign/561167-jd-vance-jumps-into-ohio-senate-primary/|first=Tal|last=Axelrod}}</ref> Vance was endorsed by former [[President of the United States|president]] [[Donald Trump]] late in the primary.<ref>{{cite news|title=Trump endorses J.D. Vance, wading into Ohio's contentious Republican Senate primary|date=April 15, 2022|first=Eric|last=McDaniel|newspaper=NPR|url=https://www.npr.org/2022/04/15/1093148912/trump-endorses-j-d-vance-wading-into-ohios-contentious-republican-senate-primary}}</ref>

U.S. representative and [[Tim Ryan 2020 presidential campaign|2020 presidential candidate]], [[Tim Ryan (Ohio politician)|Tim Ryan]], was the Democratic nominee.

| before_party = Republican Party (United States)
| after_election = [[James Lankford]]
| after_party = Republican Party (United States)
}}
{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Oklahoma}}

One-term Republican [[James Lankford]] won the [[2014 United States Senate special election in Oklahoma|2014 special election]] to serve the remainder of former U.S. senator [[Tom Coburn]]'s term.<ref>{{cite web |title=Federal, State, Legislative, and Judicial Races General Election — November 4, 2014 |url=https://oklahoma.gov/elections/support/2014/20141104-seb.html |website=Oklahoma.gov |publisher=Oklahoma State Election Board |access-date=March 25, 2021 |archive-date=April 8, 2021 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210408200906/https://oklahoma.gov/elections/support/2014/20141104-seb.html }}</ref> Lankford won election to his first full term in [[2016 United States Senate election in Oklahoma|2016]] with 67.7% of the vote. He announced that he would be running for re-election on April 6, 2021. Two Democratic candidates were competing in the runoff Democratic primary election.<ref>{{cite web |title=Federal, State, Legislative, and Judicial Races General Election — November 8, 2016 |url=https://oklahoma.gov/elections/support/2016/20161108-seb.html |website=Oklahoma.gov |publisher=Oklahoma State Election Board |access-date=March 25, 2021 |archive-date=April 8, 2021 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210408195630/https://oklahoma.gov/elections/support/2016/20161108-seb.html }}</ref><ref name="Lankannounce">{{cite news |last1=Krehbiel |first1=Randy |title=Sen. Lankford says re-election bid will be about him being him |url=https://tulsaworld.com/news/state-and-regional/govt-and-politics/sen-lankford-says-reelection-bid-will-be-about-him-being-him/article_d44e312c-96dd-11eb-bcdf-53f3489449d7.html |access-date=April 7, 2021 |website=Tulsa World |date=April 6, 2021}}</ref>

Jackson Lahmeyer, the pastor for Sheridan Church and former Oklahoma state coordinator for the [[Billy Graham Evangelistic Association]], challenged Lankford in the Republican primary.<ref>{{cite news|last1=Krehbiel|first1=Randy|date=March 17, 2021|title=Tulsa pastor challenges Lankford for Senate with boost from Trump loyalist Michael Flynnglish|website=Tulsa World|url=https://tulsaworld.com/news/state-and-regional/govt-and-politics/tulsa-pastor-challenges-lankford-for-senate-with-boost-from-trump-loyalist-michael-flynn/article_0c925554-8680-11eb-a69a-bf4b63a23a15.html|access-date=March 19, 2021}}</ref>

{{Main|2022 United States Senate special election in Oklahoma}}

Five-term incumbent Republican [[Jim Inhofe]] had been re-elected with 63% of the vote in 2020 and was not scheduled to be up for election again until 2026. However, Inhofe announced his intention to resign at the end of the [[117th U.S. Congress]]. A special election to fill his seat took place in November 2022, concurrent with the other Senate elections.<ref name=Inhofe/> U.S. representative [[Markwayne Mullin]] defeated [[Oklahoma House of Representatives|state House]] speaker [[T. W. Shannon]] in the runoff Republican primary election. Mullin and Shannon defeated Inhofe's [[Chief of Staff|chief of staff]] Luke Holland and others in the initial Republican primary election.<ref name=Holland>{{cite news |title=Luke Holland, Sen. Inhofe's chief of staff, announces candidacy for US Senate |url=https://www.koco.com/article/oklahoma-inhofe-resignation-announcement-luke-holland/39224241 |publisher=KOCO |date=February 25, 2022 |access-date=February 25, 2022}}</ref><ref name=Mullin>{{cite news |last1=Morris |first1=Callie |title=Rep. Markwayne Mullin announces run for Senate |url= https://ktul.com/news/local/markwayne-mullin-announces-run-for-us-senate |publisher=KTUL News |date=February 26, 2022 |access-date=February 26, 2022}}</ref><ref>{{cite news |title=After eight years, Shannon ready for one more race |url=https://tulsaworld.com/news/state-and-regional/govt-and-politics/after-eight-years-shannon-ready-for-one-more-race/article_cd6fe3c0-a08a-11ec-a957-5b95c2684f38.html |access-date=March 10, 2022 |website=Tulsa World |date=March 10, 2022|author=Krehbiel, Randy}}</ref> Former U.S. representative [[Kendra Horn]] was the Democratic nominee, being her party's only candidate.<ref name=OK2022>{{cite news |title=OK Candidate Filing |url=https://filings.okelections.us/ViewCandidates/2022041320220415/99/all |publisher=Oklahoma State Election Board |access-date=April 15, 2022}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.oklahoman.com/story/news/2022/03/15/former-lawmaker-kendra-horn-files-jim-inhofes-u-s-senate-seat-congress/7046491001/|title=Kendra Horn files for Jim Inhofe's Senate seat|last=Casteel|first=Chris|date=March 15, 2022|access-date=March 15, 2022|website=[[The Oklahoman]]}}</ref>

Markwayne Mullin, a member of the [[Cherokee Nation]], became the first [[List of Native Americans in the United States Congress|Native American]] to serve in the U.S. Senate since fellow Republican [[Ben Nighthorse Campbell]] retired from Congress in 2005.<ref>{{Cite web  |title=Markwayne Mullin wins US Senate seat |url=https://indiancountrytoday.com/news/markwayne-mullin-wins-us-senate-seat |access-date=2022-11-12 |website=ICT |date=November 8, 2022 |language=en}}</ref>

{{Main|2022 United States Senate election in Oregon}}

{{See also|List of United States senators from Oregon|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Oregon}}
Incumbent [[Democratic Party (United States)|Democratic]] U.S. Senator [[Ron Wyden]], who was first elected in a [[1996 United States Senate special election in Oregon|1996 special election]], ran for a fifth full term.<ref name="OPB">{{Cite news |last=Ligori |first=Crystal |date=January 2, 2021 |title=US Sen. Ron Wyden will seek reelection in 2022 |work=[[Oregon Public Broadcasting]] |url=https://www.opb.org/article/2021/01/01/us-senator-wyden-seeks-reelection-2022/ |url-status=live |access-date=March 2, 2021 |archive-url=https://archive.today/20210620033743/https://www.opb.org/article/2021/01/01/us-senator-wyden-seeks-reelection-2022/ |archive-date=June 20, 2021}}</ref> [[Jo Rae Perkins]], who unsuccessfully ran for Oregon's other Senate seat in [[2020 United States Senate election in Oregon|2020]], won the [[Republican Party (United States)|Republican]] primary with 33.3% of the vote.<ref>{{Cite news |date=2022-05-17 |title=Oregon Primary Election Results |language=en-US |work=The New York Times |url=https://www.nytimes.com/interactive/2022/05/17/us/elections/results-oregon.html |access-date=2022-09-29 |issn=0362-4331}}</ref> The four candidates filing with the Oregon Secretary of State for this election included Chris Henry of the [[Oregon Progressive Party]] and Dan Pulju of the [[Pacific Green Party]].<ref>{{Citation |title=Oregon SOS Candidate Filing Search |date=2022-10-12 |url=https://secure.sos.state.or.us/orestar/CFSearchPage.do}}</ref>

Wyden ultimately won the election with 55.8% of the statewide vote. This is the first victory of Wyden's since 1996 where none of the following counties went Democratic in a Senate Class III election: [[Gilliam County, Oregon|Gilliam]], [[Jackson County, Oregon|Jackson]], [[Marion County, Oregon|Marion]], [[Polk County, Oregon|Polk]], [[Wasco County, Oregon|Wasco]] and [[Yamhill County, Oregon|Yamhill]]. It is also the first time [[Columbia County, Oregon|Columbia County]] supported the Republican nominee over Wyden.<ref>{{Cite web |date=2022-11-08 |title=Wyden wins reelection, will return for 5th full Senate term |url=https://www.koin.com/news/elections/general-election-us-senate-seat-ron-wyden-jo-rae-perkins-11082022/ |access-date=2022-11-09 |website=KOIN.com |language=en-US |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20221109225149/https://www.koin.com/news/elections/general-election-us-senate-seat-ron-wyden-jo-rae-perkins-11082022/ |archive-date=November 9, 2022 |url-status=dead}}</ref>

{{See also|List of United States senators from Pennsylvania|2022 United States House of Representatives elections in Pennsylvania}}
Two-term Republican [[Pat Toomey]] was [[2016 United States Senate election in Pennsylvania|re-elected in 2016]] with 48.8% of the vote. On October 5, 2020, Toomey announced that he would retire at the end of his term.<ref name="toomeyretires"/>

Lieutenant Governor [[John Fetterman]]<ref>{{cite news |last1=Otterbein |first1=Holly |title=John Fetterman launches Senate bid in Pennsylvania |url=https://www.politico.com/news/2021/02/08/fetterman-senate-pennsylvania-466932 |access-date=February 8, 2021 |work=[[Politico]] |date=February 8, 2021}}</ref> easily defeated state representative [[Malcolm Kenyatta]] and U.S. representative [[Conor Lamb]] in the Democratic primary.<ref name="politico.com">{{cite web|title=Conor Lamb launching Senate bid in Pennsylvania|url= https://www.politico.com/news/2021/08/06/conor-lamb-senate-pennsylvania-502666|archive-url= https://web.archive.org/web/20210806113852/https://www.politico.com/news/2021/08/06/conor-lamb-senate-pennsylvania-502666|url-status= dead|archive-date= August 6, 2021|access-date=August 6, 2021|website=Politico|date=August 6, 2021|author=Arkin, James}}</ref>

[[Mehmet Oz]], host of ''[[The Dr. Oz Show]]'' and [[cardiothoracic surgeon]],<ref>{{cite news|url = https://www.inquirer.com/news/dr-oz-joins-pa-senate-race-20211130.html|title = Dr. Oz officially joins the Senate race in Pennsylvania|work = [[The Philadelphia Inquirer]]|date = November 30, 2021|access-date = November 30, 2021|last = Tamari|first = Jonathan}}</ref> narrowly defeated business executive [[Dave McCormick]], 2018 U.S. Senate candidate [[Jeff Bartos]],<ref>{{cite web |last1=Tamari |first1=Jonathan |title=Real estate developer Jeff Bartos launches a Republican Senate campaign in Pennsylvania |url=https://www.inquirer.com/politics/election/jeff-bartos-2022-pennsylvania-republican-senate-race-20210308.html |website=Philadelphia Inquirer |publisher=The Philadelphia Inquirer, LLC |access-date=March 9, 2021|date=March 8, 2021}}</ref> 2018 candidate for {{ushr|Pennsylvania|7}} Sean Gale,<ref>{{cite web |last1=Brennan |first1=Chris |date=February 16, 2021|title=The Gale brothers of Montgomery County are teaming up to run for governor and U.S. Senate |url=https://www.inquirer.com/politics/election/joe-gale-2022-pennsylvania-governor-race-20210216.html |website=Philadelphia Inquirer |publisher=The Philadelphia Inquirer, LLC. |access-date=March 9, 2021}}</ref> political commentator [[Kathy Barnette]],<ref>{{cite web |last1=Cole |first1=John |title=Barnette, Former PA4 Nominee and Conservative Commentator, Announces 2022 U.S. Senate Bid |url=https://www.politicspa.com/barnette-former-pa4-nominee-and-conservative-commentator-announces-2022-u-s-senate-bid/97858/ |website=PoliticsPA |access-date=April 7, 2021|date=April 6, 2021}}</ref> and former [[List of ambassadors of the United States to Denmark|U.S. ambassador to Denmark]] [[Carla Sands]],<ref>{{cite news|last=Owens|first=Dennis|date=July 13, 2021|title=Cumberland Valley
